KAMAdemoiſelle De Bonrk (Daughter of Count 
De Bourk and of the Counteſs his Lady, 
whoſe Father, Marquis De Varenne, Lieut. Gen. 


of the King's Armies, Governor of | Bouchain, 


heretofore Commandant of Metz, and allied to 
the beſt Families in Paris) had ſent away ſeveral

whereof, as alſo of her Deliverance, 1 deem 
myſelf obliged to publiſh the intire Narrative. 

The Counteſs De Bourk, deſirous of mak-

a Native of Ireland (his Catholic Majeſty's Em- 


daſſador-Extraordinary to the Court of Sweden, 
and now at Madrid) demanded and obtained 4

her Sons, between 3 and 4 Years old, whom ſhe left 


with her Mother the Marchioneſs De Varenne.

quis De Varenne, her Brother, a Sea-Officer, who 
accompanied her to Montpellier, where ſhe was 
diſſuaded from going by Land thro' the French

League, without the Gulf or Bay, but he 
paid dearly for his Imprudence. He was there 
attacked with a contrary Wind which he was 
not able to maſter, and which drove him back 
again upon the Coaſt. He had Recourſe to Oars 
but the Weakneſs of his Equipage rendered them 
inefteQual, and, Spite of all his Efforts, the Tar- 
tan ſtruck upon a Rock and bilged. The whole 
Stern was immediately under Water, and M. De 
Bourk, being, with her Son and three Female 
Attendants, in the Cabbin, were drowned. 
Thoſe who were at the Head, among whom were 
M. L' Abb De Bourk , Mr Artbur ; the Lady's 
Steward; a Maid-ſervant, and the Lacquey, 
clung ts the Wreck which lay on the Rock.

M. De Bourk, whom he drew out and put

M. L' Abbe De Bourk was the firſt who went down

boring dry Shoal, whence he had till a narrow 
Armof the Sea to paſs before he could get aſhore. 
He catched at a dry Plank, torn from the Wreck, 
but it was driven away out of his Hands. At 
length, making uſe of one of the Tartan's Oars, 
he gained a Rock adjoining to the Conti- 

nent. | 
Thoſe Moors, who were in that Fart of the 
Strand, not only tore and cut off all his Cloaths,

The Time of its Foundation was in 1551. Its 
founder Vas E Sebaſtian Del Puerto, a Religious 
of the Convent at Burger, in Spain, Having, 
thro' hisexceſſrve Zeal for the Redemption of. 
zptives; amaſſed a Quantity of Alms, he firſt 
paſſed over to Algiers in 1536 *, where he ran- 
med two hundred Slaves: And, being touched 
ith the Miſeries wherein he beheld thoſe who 
ere afflicted with Sickneſs, after having begged 
p and down, and collected {till larger Sums, he 
rent over and put the firſt Hand to this metito= 
ous Eſtabliſhment. He returned a third Time 
with the Emperor Charles V, who admitted 
im among his Counſellors. He is reported to 
have predicted to that Monarch the Deſtruction 
f his Armada 44. After theſe charitable Exer- 
iſes, he died, in 1556, loaded with Years and 
Merit, 4 7725 | *

lemptions, -when a Piece of News brought from 
brfica put a Stop to their Career, A Daughter of

e City of Algiers, named Ay the 
Turks (” a1 ge Treir by He Mo, 
Capital of the Kingdom of that Nine, bs fithtatt 
in 36 Degrees, 48 Minutes N. Latitude, 4nd 
24 Degrees 30 Minutes of Longitude. It 
lies on the Declivity of a Mountain. Its Build. 
ings, all terraſſed and white, render its Diſto- 
very agreeable to the Sight. As for myſelf, in 
approaching the Port, it ſeemed to me as 
ſpacious Amphitheater, wherein the Faith and 
Conſtancy of ſo many Captives, the Zeal ant 
Charity of ſo many of our good Fathers, the 
Religion and Perſeverance of ſo many Chriftiam, 
have ſo long combated againſt' Barbarian, 
worſe than Lions and Tigers, and have aug. 
mented the Number of Martyrs. The Vene. 
ration for a Place which they had watered with 
their Blood, and illuſtrated by a thouſand Fer 
Examples of Piety and Firmneſs, inſpired me 
with a pious Impatience to be there, and to 
gather ſome Sparks of that glorious Fire by 
which they were animated. i 
Its Walls, waſhed by the Sea Waves iin. 
ſpired me with a Reverence to even thoſe;

they-wauld..not renounce their, Faith * In

know ſo much of the Place and People, that 
happy would it have been for poor Tremi- 
gan, had neither Tur nor Spaniards. ever 
beheld it. The bare Sight of the wretched, 
tho“ noble and venerable Remains of that 
once large and beautiful City, have more 
than once brought Tears to my Eyes; and 
the doleful Stories told me by ſome of her 
ancient Citizens have ſet them flowing. 
Without Partiality, to give my own Opinion 
of. People to whom I am not quite a Stranger, 
I muſt affirm, That even under the Algerine 
Tarks, Vaſſals live much happier than any, 


eſpecially if they are not native Spaniards, - 


can do, who are abſolutely ſuch to the Sa- 


niards. What the aue has in Ia ſdleuce, the 


other, in a moſt abundant Meaſure, has in 


Arrogance. For Avarice, I fancy, they 


may ſhake Hands. For Cruelty, the one 
will hold his Hand on humble Submiſſion, 


which I ſeldom find the other will do. And 


as to hat the Spaniard may, perhaps, come 


hort of the Algerine in downright: Beaſtia- 


lity, is made confounded Amends for in hit 


Ifery Catholic Zeal and inſupportable Bi- 
gotry. Tho it is certain, and very obvious, 


that ſome of the Natives of Barbary have

Benefits were far from being inconſiderable.. It has been 
allowed, that our Armies, and thoſe of our Allies, in 
Sam 'muſt infallibly have periſhed, had they not beet 
ſupported from Barbary; and Oran is none of the worſt 
Barbary Corn-Markets. Our Gibraltar and Port- Mabos 
(long may they remain ours) had conſtantly a great Por- 
tion of theit daily Subſiſtence from this very Market. 
But what inhances our, Obligation to that Place, and its 
late Owners, was; That no other Nation whatever was 
ſuffered. to have a Reſident there, or indeed any Trade 
at all. The French long urged for a Share in that im- 
portant Commerce; bat, to my own cettain and poſi- 
tive Knowledge could never obtain even 4 patient 
Hearing. Indeed, about a Year fince (as I am inform- 
ed by the Agerine Envoy) ey did get thither a Sort 
of gent, who, in Requital, has (it ſeems) returned 
the Turi the worſt ot Offices he could have done them, 
as I hall preſently obſerve, If all this was not ſome 
Advantage to us, in particular, I know what will be 
counted fluch. But let us draw towards the Concluſion ' 

of our CONCLUSION. | 
Abdi Baſha, the preſent Dey of Algiers (whom one 
of our pleaſant Sea-Captains, who ſaw him not long 
fiace, calls his Monoculoxs Highneſs) has fince May, 
1724) _—_ that Dignity, with general _— 
e is an Old- ſtander there, and has gradually paſſed thro* 
ſeveral of the moſt important Charges of the Govern- 
ment r' Bay or Vice-Roy of the Southern 
Province, and Age or General of the Arab Cavalry; 
which latter beneficial Poſt he had held ſome Years, f 
doubt whether the Algerizes ever had a Chief ſo uni- 
yerſally beloved, or ſo capable of governing that unruly 
Militia. His Example is good, his Diſpoſition mild and 
benevolent, yet withal a ſtrict Lover of Joſtice,and can be 
very ſtern upon a proper Occaſion : All which good 
Qualities render him very eſtimable, even in the Eyes of 
the Natives, who ſeldom bear any hearty good Will to 
their imperious Maſters the Tzrks. As to his Perſon, 
he is a large · timbered, robuſt Man, upwards of Sixty, 
having loſt one of his Eyes by the Small-Pox, and, as 
I hear, continues to wear very well; which is no Won-
